=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a 2D integer array intervals, where intervals[i] = [l_i, r_i, weight_i]. Interval i starts at position l_i and ends at r_i, and has a weight of weight_i. You can choose up to 4 non-overlapping intervals. The score of the chosen intervals is defined as the total sum of their weights.
Return the lexicographically smallest array of at most 4 indices from intervals with maximum score, representing your choice of non-overlapping intervals.
Two intervals are said to be non-overlapping if they do not share any points. In particular, intervals sharing a left or right boundary are considered overlapping.
An array a is lexicographically smaller than an array b if in the first position where a and b differ, array a has an element that is less than the corresponding element in b.
If the first min(a.length, b.length) elements do not differ, then the shorter array is the lexicographically smaller one.
 
Example 1:

Input: intervals = [[1,3,2],[4,5,2],[1,5,5],[6,9,3],[6,7,1],[8,9,1]]
Output: [2,3]
Explanation:
You can choose the intervals with indices 2, and 3 with respective weights of 5, and 3.

Example 2:

Input: intervals = [[5,8,1],[6,7,7],[4,7,3],[9,10,6],[7,8,2],[11,14,3],[3,5,5]]
Output: [1,3,5,6]
Explanation:
You can choose the intervals with indices 1, 3, 5, and 6 with respective weights of 7, 6, 3, and 5.

 
Constraints:

1 <= intevals.length <= 5 * 10^4
intervals[i].length == 3
intervals[i] = [l_i, r_i, weight_i]
1 <= l_i <= r_i <= 10^9
1 <= weight_i <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

def insert_idx(tup, idx):
    # tup sorted ascending
    # return tuple sorted
    # small, linear
    for pos, val in enumerate(tup):
        if idx < val:
            return tup[:pos] + (idx,) + tup[pos:]
    return tup + (idx,)

Let's draft code:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(intervals)
        arr = []
        for idx, (l, r, w) in enumerate(intervals):
            arr.append((r, l, w, idx))
        arr.sort()  # sort by r, then l, then w, then idx. Is sorting by l/w okay? It doesn't affect p? It affects processing order among same r. Does arbitrary order among same r matter? We said no. But sorting by l/w might affect dp? It should not affect final because same r intervals mutually exclusive and all processed before future. But does order among same r affect p_i for intervals with same r? p_i excludes all same r, so include option for later same r cannot use earlier same r. Exclude option dp[c][i-1] accumulates best among earlier same r. Processing all same r eventually considers all. Order arbitrary. Sorting by tuple (r,l,w,idx) is deterministic. Good.
        rs = [x[0] for x in arr]
        NEG = -10**30
        dp = [[(NEG, ()) for _ in range(n + 1)] for _ in range(5)]
        for i in range(n + 1):
            dp[0][i] = (0, ())
        
        def insert_sorted(tup, idx):
            # tup sorted
            for pos, val in enumerate(tup):
                if idx < val:
                    return tup[:pos] + (idx,) + tup[pos:]
            return tup + (idx,)
        
        def better(a, b):
            if a[0] != b[0]:
                return a if a[0] > b[0] else b
            return a if a[1] < b[1] else b
        
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            r, l, w, idx = arr[i-1]
            p = bisect_left(rs, l)
            for c in range(1, 5):
                best = dp[c][i-1]
                prev = dp[c-1][p]
                if prev[0] != NEG:
                    cand = (prev[0] + w, insert_sorted(prev[1], idx))
                    if cand[0] > best[0] or (cand[0] == best[0] and cand[1] < best[1]):
                        best = cand
                dp[c][i] = best
        
        ans = dp[0][n]
        for c in range(1, 5):
            cand = dp[c][n]
            if cand[0] > ans[0] or (cand[0] == ans[0] and cand[1] < ans[1]):
                ans = cand
        return list(ans[1])

Let's attempt to prove DP with lex tie more formally in case there is hidden flaw.

We want max score among all subsets of size <=4. Sort intervals by r. Define S(c, i) = set of all valid subsets of exactly c intervals chosen from first i intervals in sorted order. Define value V(c,i) = (max score, lex smallest sorted index tuple among those with max score). Recurrence:
For i>0, any subset in S(c,i) either does not include interval i, then in S(c,i-1); or includes interval i, then the other c-1 intervals must be from first p_i intervals (r < l_i) and form subset in S(c-1,p_i). Conversely, any subset from S(c,i-1) or any subset from S(c-1,p_i) plus i is valid. Thus max score and lex tuple can be computed by comparing these two options. This is standard.

Tie-breaking: For option not include, value = V(c,i-1). For include, value = (score(V(c-1,p_i)) + w_i, insert_sorted(tuple(V(c-1,p_i)), idx_i)). Need show choosing V(c-1,p_i) (lex smallest among max score) yields lex smallest include option. Suppose another state T in S(c-1,p_i) has same score but lex larger. Adding i to T yields tuple insert(T, idx_i). Since V and T have same length c-1, and V lex < T, insertion monotonic for equal length, so insert(V, idx_i) lex < insert(T, idx_i). If T has lower score, include score lower. Thus V is optimal. Good. For comparing not include vs include, both have length c, so lex compare direct. Thus recurrence valid.

Final answer: max over c of V(c,n) with score primary, lex tuple secondary. Need compare different lengths. Suppose two states have same score. Could one tuple be prefix of another? If tuple A is prefix of tuple B, then B contains all intervals in A plus extra intervals, so score B > score A because weights positive. Contradiction same score. Thus no prefix. But even if prefix, Python tuple comparison gives shorter smaller, which matches problem. So final compare okay. Actually if same score and prefix impossible, but if it did, shorter lex smaller; Python would choose shorter. Good.

Now, let's think about if insertion monotonic for equal length is enough. We proved intuitively. Could there be a counterexample with equal length and duplicate values? Indices distinct, so no duplicates within tuple, but x could be less/greater. Let's formally test with brute mental for length 2 all possible orderings.

Let A=(a1,a2), B=(b1,b2), sorted, A<B. Cases:
- a1 < b1. Insert x.
   If x < a1: A'=(x,a1,a2), B'=(x,b1,b2); compare x=x, then a1<b1 => A'<B'.
   If x = a1? impossible if x not in A; x could equal a1? no because x is new index not in A. But if x equals a1 impossible. If a1 < x < b1: A'=(a1,x,a2), B'=(x,b1,b2); compare a1 < x => A'<B'.
   If x > b1: A'=(a1,a2,x), B'=(b1,b2,x); compare a1<b1 => A'<B'.
   If x between a2 and? If x > b1 but maybe x < a2? Since a2 could be > b1? Wait A sorted, a2 >= a1. B sorted, b2 >= b1. If a1 < b1, x > b1. Then x > a1. Insert in A after a1, maybe before or after a2. Insert in B after b1. Compare first elements a1 vs b1, A'<B'. Good.
- a1 = b1, then compare a2 < b2 (since A<B and same length, no prefix). Insert x.
   If x < a1: A'=(x,a1,a2), B'=(x,b1,b2); compare a1=b1, a2<b2 => A'<B'.
   If x = a1? impossible. If a1 < x < a2: A'=(a1,x,a2), B'=(a1,x,b2) if x < b2? Since x < a2 < b2, yes; compare a1=a1, x=x, a2<b2 => A'<B'. If a2 < x < b2: A'=(a1,a2,x), B'=(a1,x,b2); compare a1=a1, a2 < x => A'<B'. If x > b2: A'=(a1,a2,x), B'=(a1,b2,x); compare a2<b2 => A'<B'. Good.
So monotonic for length 2. For length up to 4, similar.

Let's maybe write final code with local variables for speed:

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(intervals)
        arr = []
        for idx, (l, r, w) in enumerate(intervals):
            arr.append((r, l, w, idx))
        arr.sort()
        rs = [x[0] for x in arr]
        NEG = -10**30
        dp = [[(NEG, ()) for _ in range(n + 1)] for _ in range(5)]
        empty = (0, ())
        for i in range(n + 1):
            dp[0][i] = empty

        def insert_sorted(tup, idx):
            for pos, val in enumerate(tup):
                if idx < val:
                    return tup[:pos] + (idx,) + tup[pos:]
            return tup + (idx,)

        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            _, l, w, idx = arr[i - 1]
            p = bisect_left(rs, l)
            for c in range(1, 5):
                best = dp[c][i - 1]
                prev = dp[c - 1][p]
                if prev[0] != NEG:
                    cand = (prev[0] + w, insert_sorted(prev[1], idx))
                    if cand[0] > best[0] or (cand[0] == best[0] and cand[1] < best[1]):
                        best = cand
                dp[c][i] = best

        ans = dp[0][n]
        for c in range(1, 5):
            cand = dp[c][n]
            if cand[0] > ans[0] or (cand[0] == ans[0] and cand[1] < ans[1]):
                ans = cand
        return list(ans[1])

Need note: In loop, `_, l, w, idx = arr[i - 1]`; arr tuple is (r,l,w,idx), so first element r ignored. Good.

Potential issue: `empty = (0, ())` and `dp[0][i] = empty` for all i; dp[0] list initially invalid, then overwritten. Good.

Potential issue: `dp = [[(NEG, ()) for _ in range(n + 1)] for _ in range(5)]` creates 5 lists; dp[0] overwritten. Good.

Potential issue: If `prev[0] != NEG` but prev is invalid with score NEG + w? We never create invalid with non-NEG. Good.

Potential issue: If `best` is invalid and `cand` valid, condition cand[0] > best[0] true. Good.

Potential issue: If `best` valid and `cand` invalid? We skip cand if prev invalid, so no cand. Good.

Potential issue: If `prev` valid but adding interval i creates a tuple with duplicate idx? Could prev tuple contain idx? prev is from first p intervals sorted by r, where p <= i-1, so interval i not in prev. Original index unique, so no duplicate. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "arr.sort()" sorting by r then l then w then idx; if two intervals have same r and l but different w, and one has smaller index but lower weight, dp for c=1 chooses higher weight. If same score? no. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "score" if weights are positive but maybe selecting a set with fewer intervals can have same score as more intervals; final compare. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "dp[c][i]" if c=4 and i=n, if there are fewer than 4 non-overlapping intervals, invalid. final won't choose invalid because score NEG. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "arr" storing `idx` from enumerate, but if input intervals is a list of lists, enumerate yields int; okay.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "final answer" if `ans` is a tuple and we return list(ans[1]); if ans[1] is tuple of ints, list of ints. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "time" due to `bisect_left` inside loop for each interval; O(n log n). Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "space" due to `rs` list of r values; okay.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "intervals" if length 5*10^4 and weights up to 1e9, score up to 4e9; Python int. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "lexicographically smallest array" if arrays are compared as arrays of integers and shorter prefix smaller; Python tuple comparison. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "dp[c][i]" if a state with same score but lex smaller has a larger max r than another state, and future interval j has l_j between max r of lex smaller and max r of other? But if both states are in dp[c-1][p_j], then p_j = count r < l_j, so all intervals in prefix have r < l_j. Thus max r of any state in prefix < l_j. So no state in prefix has max r >= l_j. But if both states are in dp[c-1][p_j], then p_j = count r < l_j, so all intervals in prefix have r < l_j. Thus max r of any state in prefix < l_j. So no state in prefix has max r >= l_j. If we were using dp[c-1][i] for i > p_j, not the case. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "dp[c][i]" if a state with same score but lex smaller has a larger max r than another state, and future interval j has l_j between max r of lex smaller and max r of other? But if both states are in dp[c-1][p_j], then p_j = count r < l_j, so all intervals in prefix have r < l_j. Thus max r of any state in prefix < l_j. So no state in prefix has max r >= l_j. If we were using dp[c-1][i] for i > p_j, not the case. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "dp[c][i]" if a state with same score but lex smaller has a larger max r than another state, and future interval j has l_j between max r of lex smaller and max r of other? But if both states are in dp[c-1][p_j], then p_j = count r < l_j, so all intervals in prefix have r < l_j. Thus max r of any state in prefix < l_j. So no state in prefix has max r >= l_j. If we were using dp[c-1][i] for i > p_j, not the case. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "dp[c][i]" if a state with same score but lex smaller has a larger max r than another state, and future interval j has l_j between max r of lex smaller and max r of other? But if both states are in dp[c-1][p_j], then p_j = count r < l_j, so all intervals in prefix have r < l_j. Thus max r of any state in prefix < l_j. So no state in prefix has max r >= l_j. If we were using dp[c-1][i] for i > p_j, not the case. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
. Let's output only the code.</think>```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(intervals)
        
        arr = []
        for idx, (l, r, w) in enumerate(intervals):
            arr.append((r, l, w, idx))
        arr.sort()
        
        rs = [x[0] for x in arr]
        NEG = -10**30
        
        dp = [[(NEG, ()) for _ in range(n + 1)] for _ in range(5)]
        empty = (0, ())
        for i in range(n + 1):
            dp[0][i] = empty
        
        def insert_sorted(tup: tuple, idx: int) -> tuple:
            for pos, val in enumerate(tup):
                if idx < val:
                    return tup[:pos] + (idx,) + tup[pos:]
            return tup + (idx,)
        
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            _, l, w, idx = arr[i - 1]
            p = bisect_left(rs, l)
            
            for c in range(1, 5):
                best = dp[c][i - 1]
                prev = dp[c - 1][p]
                
                if prev[0] != NEG:
                    cand = (prev[0] + w, insert_sorted(prev[1], idx))
                    if cand[0] > best[0] or (cand[0] == best[0] and cand[1] < best[1]):
                        best = cand
                
                dp[c][i] = best
        
        ans = dp[0][n]
        for c in range(1, 5):
            cand = dp[c][n]
            if cand[0] > ans[0] or (cand[0] == ans[0] and cand[1] < ans[1]):
                ans = cand
        
        return list(ans[1])
```
